\overline{G}=\overline{{\rm GL}}_{2},\ \overline{G}_{0}=\overline{G}_{\rm der}\text{ and }\overline{Z}_{0}=\overline{Z(G)},

we carried out an extensive study in
[
GSS2
]
. For the induction, the representation
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{Z(G)}\cdot\overline{G}_{\rm der}}^{\overline{G}}(\rho)
is not an isotypic sum in general; in fact, it is useful already to determine when it is irreducible. For double covers of
{\rm GSp}_{2r}
, this was investigated in
[
Szp4-1
,
Szp5
]
.

2.3.1.
An irreducibility criterion

Let
\overline{G}
be an
n
-fold cover of
G
. We continue to assume that
\overline{Z(G)}
is associated with a sublattice
Y_{G,c}=\mathbf{Z}e_{c}\subset Y
. Henceforth, we will denote

\overline{G}_{0}:=\overline{G}_{\rm der},

whenever there is no possibility of confusion. Let
\overline{M}\subset\overline{G}
be a covering Levi subgroup. Define

\overline{M}_{0}:=\overline{M}\cap\overline{G}_{\rm der},

which is then a Levi subgroup of
\overline{G}_{\rm der}
. We have

\overline{M}_{\rm der}\subset\overline{M}_{0}\subset\overline{M},

where the first inclusion however may not be an equality in general. We consider

\overline{M}^{\dagger}:=\overline{Z(G)}\cdot\overline{M}_{0}.

There are inclusions of normal subgroups

\overline{M}^{\dagger}\subset\overline{Z(M)}\cdot\overline{M}_{0}\subset\overline{M}

with
\overline{M}/\overline{M}^{\dagger}
a finite abelian group. The following result is straightforward.

Lemma 2.10
.

For every Levi subgroup
M\subset G
, one has
\overline{M}/\overline{M}^{\dagger}\simeq\overline{T}/\overline{T}^{\dagger}
; in particular, the quotient is independent of the Levi subgroup.

From now on, we write

\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}:=\overline{T}/\overline{T}^{\dagger}.

We are interested in both the induction and restriction of representations between
\overline{M}^{\dagger}
and
\overline{M}
. It is natural to expect that any answer will depend on the specific representation chosen. Nonetheless, for special covering groups, it is possible to obtain a uniform description of the induction and restriction process, to describe the irreducbility of an induction and the decomposition from a restriction. We briefly explain the main idea following
[
Szp4-1
]
.

The two groups
\overline{Z(G)}
and
\overline{M}_{0}
have a finite intersection
\overline{Z(G)}\cap\overline{M}_{0}
which lies in the center of both groups. Thus,
\overline{M}^{\dagger}
is almost a direct product of
\overline{Z(G)}
and
\overline{M}_{0}
. Every representation of
\overline{M}^{\dagger}
is of the form

\tau\boxtimes\rho,

where
\tau\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{Z(G)})
and
\rho\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{M}_{0})
are compatible, i.e., they agree on the intersection
\overline{Z(G)}\cap\overline{M}_{0}
. Thus,

\tau\boxtimes\rho\simeq\tau^{\prime}\boxtimes\rho^{\prime}

if and only if

\tau\simeq\tau^{\prime}\text{ and }\rho\simeq\rho^{\prime}.

Denote by
\omega_{\tau}
the central character of
\tau
, and similarly
\omega_{\rho}
for
\rho
. Then we can use the central characters
\omega_{\tau}
or
\omega_{\rho}
to distinguish representations. More precisely, if
\omega_{\tau}\neq\omega_{\tau^{\prime}}
or
\omega_{\rho}\neq\omega_{\rho^{\prime}}
, then
\tau\boxtimes\rho
and
\tau^{\prime}\boxtimes\rho^{\prime}
are not isomorphic. In fact, since
\overline{Z(G)}
is a Heisenberg-type group, one has

\tau\simeq\tau^{\prime}\text{ if and only if }\omega_{\tau}=\omega_{\tau^{\prime}}.

We denote

\mathfrak{Q}_{M}:=Z(\overline{M}^{\dagger})/Z(\overline{M})

and

\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}:=Z(\overline{Z(G)})/Z(\overline{G}),\quad\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}:=Z(\overline{M}_{0})/(\overline{M}_{0}\cap Z(\overline{M})).

Note that here
\overline{M}_{0}\cap Z(\overline{M})=Z(\overline{M}_{0})\cap Z(\overline{M})
. The natural surjective map

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}\times\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}_{M}}

has a finite kernel. Let

\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M}}:={\rm Hom}(\mathfrak{Q}_{M},\mathbf{C}^{\times}),\ \widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}:={\rm Hom}(\mathfrak{Q}_{Z},\mathbf{C}^{\times})\text{ and }\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}:={\rm Hom}(\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}},\mathbf{C}^{\times})

be the Pontryagin duals. One has an embedding

(2.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}\times\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}.}

There is a natural conjugation action of
\overline{M}
on
\mathfrak{Q}_{Z},\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{M}
, and thus also on their Pontryagin duals
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}},\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M}}
respectively. Since
\overline{M}^{\dagger}
acts trivially on all
\mathfrak{Q}_{Z},\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{M}
, we have a well-defined action of
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
on the three Pontryagin duals.
The embedding in (
2.9
) is
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
-equivariant with respect to this natural action.

It is clear that the action of
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
(resp.
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}
) is trivial if and only if
Z(\overline{Z(G)})\subset Z(\overline{T})
(resp.
Z(\overline{M}_{0})\subset Z(\overline{T})
). If
Z(\overline{M}_{0})
is associated with a sublattice
Y_{M_{0},c}\subset Y
, then it follows from Lemma
2.7
that these two inclusions are equivalent to

Y_{G,c,Q,n}\subset Y_{Q,n}

and

(Y_{M_{0},c}\cap Y_{0,Q,n})\subset Y_{Q,n}

respectively. Here
Y_{G,c,Q,n}\subset Y_{G,c}
is the sublattice given by (
2.5
) with respect to the restriction of
B_{Q}
on
Y_{G,c}
, and
Y_{0}=Y^{sc}
is the cocharacter lattice of
G_{0}=G_{\rm der}
.

Theorem 2.11
.

Keep notations as above.

(i)

Suppose the action of
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
on either
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
or
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}
is free. Then
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{M}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{M}}(\tau\boxtimes\rho)
is irreducible for every
\tau\boxtimes\rho\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{M}^{\dagger})
.

(ii)

Specializing to the case
M=T
, if the action of
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
on both
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{T}}
are trivial, then for every
\pi\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{T})
we have

\pi|_{\overline{T}^{\dagger}}=(\tau\boxtimes\rho)^{\oplus e}

for some

e\in\mathbf{N}
. On the other hand, if
Z(\overline{T})\subset Z(\overline{Z(G)})\cdot Z(\overline{T}_{0})
, then
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{T}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{T}}(\tau\boxtimes\rho)
is an isotypic sum of an irreducible representation of
\overline{T}
.

Proof.

For (i), in view of (
2.9
), the assumption implies that the action of
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M}}
is free. Thus, the assertion is a direct consequence of Mackey’s irreducibility criterion for induced representations.

For the first assertion in (ii), let
(\tau\boxtimes\rho)\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{T}^{\dagger})
be a constituent of
\pi|_{\overline{T}^{\dagger}}
. The assumption implies that

S_{\tau\boxtimes\rho}=\overline{T}

and thus by the discussion in §
2.2.1
the restriction
\pi|_{\overline{T}^{\dagger}}
is an isotypic sum. We give an alternate argument as follows. The assumption implies that

(2.10)

Z(\overline{Z(G)})\cdot Z(\overline{T}_{0})\subset Z(\overline{T}).

The representation
\pi
is determined by its central character
\omega_{\pi}
. Every constituent
\tau\boxtimes\rho\subset\pi|_{\overline{T}^{\dagger}}
is also determined by
\omega_{\tau}
and
\omega_{\rho}
. However, the inclusion (
2.10
) implies that
\omega_{\tau}
and
\omega_{\rho}
are uniquely determined by
\omega_{\pi}
. That is,
\pi|_{\overline{T}^{\dagger}}
is an isotypic sum in this case.

For the second assertion in (ii), it is exactly the same argument as the above with the inclusion in (
2.10
) reversed. Indeed, every constituent
\pi
of
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{T}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{T}}(\tau\boxtimes\rho)
is determined by its central character
\omega_{\pi}
. Since
Z(\overline{T})\subset Z(\overline{Z(G)})\cdot Z(\overline{T}_{0})
, we have

\omega_{\pi}=(\omega_{\tau}\cdot\omega_{\rho})|_{Z(\overline{T})},

which is uniquely determined by
\tau\boxtimes\rho
. Thus, in this case
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{T}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{T}}(\tau\boxtimes\rho)=\pi^{\oplus e}
for some
e\in\mathbf{N}
.
∎

Corollary 2.12
.

The following three statements are equivalent:

–

\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
acts trivially on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{T_{0}}}
;

–

the inclusion
Z(\overline{T}_{0})\subset Z(\overline{T})
holds;

–

the equality
Y_{0}\cap Y_{Q,n}=Y_{0,Q,n}
holds.

If one (and thus every) of the above holds, then for every
\pi\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{T})
the restriction
\pi|_{\overline{T}_{0}}=\rho^{\oplus e}
is an isotypic sum of a certain
\rho\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{T}_{0})
.

Proof.

The equivalence between the first two assertions follows from the definition of
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{T_{0}}}
.
Note
Y_{Q,n}=\mathbbm{L}(Z(\overline{T}))
and
Y_{0,Q,n}=\mathbbm{L}(Z(\overline{T}_{0}))
. One always has
Y_{0}\cap Y_{Q,n}\subset Y_{0,Q,n}
, which corresponds to the inclusion

\overline{T}_{0}\cap Z(\overline{T})\subset Z(\overline{T}_{0}).

This gives the equivalence between the second and the third assertions. The rest follows from the same argument as in Theorem
2.11
by noting that every irreducible genuine representation of
\overline{T}
or
\overline{T}_{0}
is determined by its central character.
∎

Now we consider two Levi subgroups
M\subset M^{\prime}\subset G
. It is easy to check that there are natural maps

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\overline{M}_{0}^{\prime})/Z(\overline{M}_{0}^{\prime})\cap Z(\overline{M})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\overline{M}_{0})/Z(\overline{M}_{0})\cap Z(\overline{M})=\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}^{\prime}}=Z(\overline{M}_{0}^{\prime})/Z(\overline{M}_{0}^{\prime})\cap Z(\overline{M}^{\prime}).}

Thus, we have a
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
-equivaraint map

\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}\longrightarrow\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}^{\prime}}}.

This immediately gives

Corollary 2.13
.

For a Levi subgroup
\overline{M}^{\prime}\subset\overline{G}
, if
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
acts freely on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}^{\prime}}}
, then:

–

\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
acts freely on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}
for every Levi subgroup
\overline{M}\subset\overline{M}^{\prime}\subset\overline{G}
;

–

hence,
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{M}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{M}}(\tau\boxtimes\rho)
is always irreducible.

2.3.2.
Reducibility of parabolic inductions

Let
(\sigma,V_{\sigma})\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{M})
, where
V_{\sigma}
is a space of realization of
\sigma
.

Let
{\bf P}={\bf M}{\bf N}\subset{\bf G}
be a parabolic subgroup associated with
\theta\subset\Delta
. One has the parabolic subgroup
\overline{P}\subset\overline{G}
by restriction from
G
to
P
. Consider the normalized induced representation

I_{\overline{P}}^{\overline{G}}(\sigma):={\rm Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\overline{G}}(\sigma)

of
\overline{G}
. Let
{\bf P}^{\prime}={\bf M}^{\prime}{\bf N}^{\prime}
be another parabolic subgroup corresponding to
\theta^{\prime}\subset\Delta
.
Define

W^{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}=\left\{w\in W:w(\theta)=\theta^{\prime}\right\}.

Call
{\bf P}
and
{\bf P}^{\prime}
associated if
W^{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset
. Let
w\in W^{\theta,\theta^{\prime}}
. We always take the representatives

\overline{w}=\overline{w}_{\alpha_{1}}...\overline{w}_{\alpha_{l}}\in\overline{G}

of
w=w_{\alpha_{1}}...w_{\alpha_{l}}
with
\overline{w}_{\alpha}
given in §
2.1
. One has
w{\bf M}w^{-1}={\bf M}^{\prime}
and thus a representation
{}^{w}\sigma
of
\overline{M}^{\prime}
given by

{}^{w}\sigma(m^{\prime})(v):=\sigma(w^{-1}mw)(v)

for any
m^{\prime}\in\overline{M}^{\prime}
and
v\in V_{\sigma}
. In particular, for the underlying vector spaces, we have
V_{{}^{w}\sigma}=V_{\sigma}
.

Consider the intertwining operator

T(w,\sigma):\ {\rm Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\overline{G}}(\sigma)\longrightarrow{\rm Ind}_{\overline{P}^{\prime}}^{\overline{G}}({}^{w}\sigma)

given by the meromorphic continuation of the integral

(2.11)

T(w,\sigma)(f)(g)=\int_{N_{w}}f(\overline{w}^{-1}ng)dn

where
N_{w}=U\cap(wN^{-}w^{-1})
with
N^{-}
the unipotent opposite
N
. Recall that we write
\overline{G}_{0}=\overline{G}_{\rm der}
whenever there is no confusion. There is a similar intertwining operator

T(w,\sigma_{0}):\ {\rm Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}(\sigma_{0})\longrightarrow{\rm Ind}_{\overline{P}^{\prime}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}({}^{w}\sigma_{0})

defined for every
\sigma_{0}\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{M}_{0})
. The following is well-known (see
[
BJ04
, Page 409]
for example).

Lemma 2.14
.

Let
\sigma\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{M})
. We have

({\rm Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\overline{G}}(\sigma))|_{\overline{G}_{0}}=\bigoplus_{\sigma_{0}\subset\sigma|_{\overline{M}_{0}}}{\rm Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}(\sigma_{0}).

On the other hand,

{\rm Ind}_{\overline{P}}^{\overline{G}}\ {\rm Ind}_{\overline{M}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{M}}(\tau\boxtimes\sigma_{0})={\rm Ind}_{\overline{G}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{G}}\big(\tau\boxtimes{\rm Ind}_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}(\sigma_{0})\big),

where
\tau\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{Z(G)})
and
\sigma_{0}\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{M}_{0})
. Moreover, the above two equalities are compatible with the standard intertwining operators
T(w,\sigma)
and
T(w,\sigma_{0})
for every
\sigma_{0}\subset\sigma|_{\overline{M}_{0}}
.

Theorem 2.15
.

Let
\overline{P}=\overline{M}N\subset\overline{G}
be a parabolic subgroup. Assume
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
acts freely on either
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
or
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}
. Let
\sigma\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{M})
, and let
\sigma_{0}\subset\sigma|_{\overline{M}_{0}}
be an irreducible constituent.

(i)

If
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
acts freely on either
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
or
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{G_{0}}}
, then
I_{\overline{P}}^{\overline{G}}(\sigma)
is irreducible if and only if
I_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}(\sigma_{0})
is irreducible.

(ii)

Assume
\sigma
is supercuspidal. If
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
acts trivially on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
and does not act freely on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{G_{0}}}
, then
I_{\overline{P}}^{\overline{G}}(\sigma)
is irreducible if and only if
I_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}(\sigma_{0})
is irreducible and

(W\cdot\sigma_{0})\cap\left\{{}^{t}(\sigma_{0}):t\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}\right\}=\left\{\sigma_{0}\right\}

holds, i.e.,
\sigma_{0}
is not Weyl-conjugate to any representation of the form
{}^{t}\sigma_{0}
with
1\neq t\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
acts freely on either
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
or
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}
, we see that

\sigma=I_{\overline{M}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{M}}(\tau\boxtimes\sigma_{0})

for some
\tau\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{Z(G)})
. Lemma
2.14
gives

(2.12)

I_{\overline{P}}^{\overline{G}}(\sigma)=I_{\overline{G}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{G}}(\tau\boxtimes I_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}(\sigma)).

Thus it is clear that if
I_{\overline{P}}^{\overline{G}}(\sigma)
is irreducible, then
I_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}(\sigma)
is irreducible.

For (i), it suffices to prove the if part. If
I_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}(\sigma)
is irreducible and
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
acts freely on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
or
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{G_{0}}}
, then
I_{\overline{P}}^{\overline{G}}(\sigma)
is irreducible by Theorem
2.11
.

For (ii), in view of Proposition
2.12
,
I_{\overline{P}}^{\overline{G}}(\sigma)
is irreducible if and only if
\tau\boxtimes I_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}(\sigma)
is not isomorphic to
({}^{t}\tau)\boxtimes{}^{t}(I_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}(\sigma_{0}))
for any
1\neq t\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
; since

{}^{t}(I_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}(\sigma_{0}))\simeq I_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}({}^{t}\sigma_{0}))

and the assumption implies that
{}^{t}\tau\simeq\tau
, this condition is equivalent to that
I_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}(\sigma_{0})
is not equivalent to
I_{\overline{P}_{0}}^{\overline{G}_{0}}({}^{t}\sigma_{0})
for any
1\neq t\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
. Again, as
\sigma
is assumed to be supercuspidal, it follows from
[
BZ2
, Theorem 2.9]
that this amounts to that
\sigma_{0}
is not in the
W
-orbit of
{}^{t}\sigma_{0}
for any
1\neq t\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
. The proof is thus completed.
∎

2.4.
Covers of
{\rm GL}_{r},{\rm GSp}_{2r}
and
{\rm GSpin}_{2r+1}

In this subsection, we work out explicitly covers of
{\rm GL}_{r},{\rm GSp}_{2r}
and
{\rm GSpin}_{2r+1}
in view of the above discussion . Some results thus generalize earlier work as in
[
Szp4-1
,
Szp5
]
, and they will also be used in later sections of the paper.

2.4.1.
Covers of
{\rm GL}_{r}

Every Brylinski–Deligne cover
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{r}
arises from two parameters
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathbf{Z}
such that

B_{Q}(e_{i},e_{j})=\begin{cases}2\mathbf{p}&\text{ if }i=j,\\
\mathbf{q}&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Here
\left\{e_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r\right\}
is a standard basis for the cocharacter lattice
Y
of
{\rm GL}_{r}
such that

\alpha_{i}^{\vee}:=e_{i}-e_{i+1},1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1

are the simple coroots. The Dyndin diagram for the simple coroots is given as follows:

One has

Q(\alpha^{\vee})=2\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}

for every coroot
\alpha^{\vee}
. There are two special families of Brylinski–Deligne covers we highlight below (see
[
Ga3
,
GW
]
):

\bullet

(Kazhdan–Patterson covers
[
KP
]
) A Kazhdan–Patterson covering
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{r,{\rm KP}}
is a Brylinski–Deligne cover
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{r}
such that

2\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}=-1.

Here
\mathbf{p}
equals the twisting parameter
c
in
[
KP
]
. We have
n_{\alpha}=n
for every root
\alpha
, and also the inclusion

nY\subset Y_{Q,n},

which however may not be an equality in general.

\bullet

(Savin covers
[
Sav2
]
) Consider the Brylinski–Deligne covers
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{r}^{(n)}
, which are associated with the pairs
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})
such that

Q(\alpha^{\vee})=2\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}=-2.

In particular, the cover parametrized by
(\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q})=(-1,0)
was first studied by Savin
[
Sav2
]
, and we denote it by
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{r,{\rm Sav}}
. In fact,
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{r,{\rm Sav}}
arises from the restriction of the
n
-fold cover of
{\rm Sp}_{2r}
to its Siegel Levi subgroup
{\rm GL}_{r}
.
The fact that
\mathbf{q}=0
accounts for the block commutativity of the covering Levi subgroups of
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{r,{\rm Sav}}
.
In this case, we have

n_{\alpha}=\frac{n}{\text{gcd}(2,n)}

and
Y_{Q,n}=n_{\alpha}\cdot Y
.

Let
{\bf r}=(r_{1},r_{2},...,r_{k})
be a partition of
r
. Let

M:=M_{\bf r}={\rm GL}_{r_{1}}\times...\times{\rm GL}_{r_{k}}\subset{\rm GL}_{r}

be the associated Levi subgroup of
{\rm GL}_{r}
. One has

M_{0}:=M_{{\bf r},0}=\left\{(g_{i}):g_{i}\in{\rm GL}_{r_{i}}\text{ and }\prod\det(g_{i})=1\right\}\subset M_{\bf r}.

In order to determine
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger},\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}
, we compute the sublattice
\mathbbm{L}\subset Y
associated with the following subgroups of
\overline{T}
:

\overline{T}^{\dagger},\ Z(\overline{Z(G)}),\ Z(\overline{G}),\ Z(\overline{M}_{0})\text{ and }\overline{M}_{0}\cap Z(\overline{M}),

whenever they exist.

First, we have

Y_{c}=\mathbf{Z}e_{c}\text{ with }e_{c}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}e_{i}.

It is clear that

\mathbbm{L}(\overline{T}^{\dagger})=Y_{c}+Y_{0}\text{ and thus }\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}\simeq e_{1}(F^{\times})/e_{1}(F^{\times r}).

An easy computation gives that

Q(e_{c})=r\mathbf{p}+\frac{r(r-1)}{2}\mathbf{q},\quad 2Q(e_{c})=r\cdot B(e_{c},e_{i})\text{ for every i},

where

B(e_{c},e_{i})=2\mathbf{p}+r(r-1)\mathbf{q}=(2\mathbf{p}+1)r-1.

It follows that

\mathbbm{L}(Z(\overline{Z(G)}))=Y_{c,Q,n}=\frac{\mathbf{Z}\cdot ne_{c}}{\gcd(n,2Q(e_{c}))}=:\mathbf{Z}(n_{1}e_{c}),

and also

\mathbbm{L}(Z(\overline{G}))=Y_{c}\cap Y_{Q,n}=\frac{\mathbf{Z}\cdot ne_{c}}{\gcd(n,B(e_{c},e_{1}))}=:\mathbf{Z}(n_{2}e_{c}).

We have

n_{2}=n_{1}\cdot\gcd(n,r),

since
r
and
B(e_{c},e_{i})
are coprime. Hence,

\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}\simeq e_{c}(F^{\times n_{1}})/e_{c}(F^{\times n_{2}}).

Recall that the action of
g\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
on
\chi_{0}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
is given by the multiplication
\chi_{0}\cdot\chi_{g}
, where

\chi_{g}:\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

with

\chi_{g}(h)=[g^{-1},h].

In the case of
{\rm GL}_{r}
, we take
g=e_{1}(a),h=e_{c}(b^{n_{1}}),a,b\in F^{\times}
to obtain

(2.13)

\chi_{e_{1}(a)}(e_{c}(b^{n_{1}}))=(b,a)_{n}^{n_{1}\cdot B_{Q}(e_{c},e_{1})}.

Two special cases to note:

–

if
n
and
r
are coprime, then
Z(\overline{Z(G)})=Z(\overline{G})
. In particular,
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
acts trivially on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
in this case.

–

if
r|n
, then it is easy to see from (
2.13
) that
\chi_{e_{1}(a)}
is trivial on
\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}
if and only if
a\in F^{\times r}
, i.e.,
e_{1}(a)=1\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
. Thus,
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
acts freely on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
. However, since in this case
\left|\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}\right|=\left|\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}\right|
, the group
\widehat{\mathfrak{O}_{Z}}
is a torsor over
\mathfrak{O}^{\dagger}
.

On the other hand, to compute
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}
, we note that

Y_{M_{0}}=Y_{0}=Y^{sc}.

Recalling that
n_{\alpha}=n/\gcd(n,Q(\alpha^{\vee}))
, if
\overline{Z(M_{0})}
is associated with a sublattice
Y_{M_{0},c}
of
Y
, then we have

Y_{M_{0},c}=Y_{0}\cap Y_{M,c},

where
Y_{M,c}
is the lattice associated with
\overline{Z(M)}
. In this case, it is easy to obtain

\mathbbm{L}(Z(\overline{M}_{0}))=Y_{M_{0},c}\cap Y_{0,Q,n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(y_{1},...,y_{1};y_{2},...,y_{2};...;y_{k},...,y_{k})\in\oplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathbf{Z}e_{i}:\\
\bullet\quad n_{\alpha}|(y_{i}-y_{i+1})\text{ for every }i,\\
\bullet\quad\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}y_{i}=0.\end{array}\right\},

where
y_{i}
appears with multiplicity
r_{i}
for every
i
. On the other hand, we have

\mathbbm{L}(\overline{M}_{0}\cap Z(\overline{M}))=Y_{M_{0},c}\cap Y_{Q,n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(y_{1},...,y_{1};y_{2},...,y_{2};...;y_{k},...,y_{k})\in\oplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathbf{Z}e_{i}:\\
\bullet\quad n_{\alpha}|y_{i}\text{ for every }i,\\
\bullet\quad\sum_{i=1}^{k}r_{i}y_{i}=0.\end{array}\right\},

For simplicity, we specialize to the case
M=T
and obtain

\mathbbm{L}(Z(\overline{T}_{0}))=Y_{0,Q,n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(y_{1},y_{2},...,y_{r})\in\oplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathbf{Z}e_{i}:\\
\bullet\quad n_{\alpha}|(y_{i}-y_{j})\text{ for every }i,j,\\
\bullet\quad\sum_{i=1}^{r}y_{i}=0.\end{array}\right\}

and

\mathbbm{L}(\overline{T}_{0}\cap Z(\overline{T}))=Y_{0}\cap Y_{Q,n}=nY_{0}.

Setting

v_{i}=e_{i}-e_{r}\text{ for }1\leqslant i\leqslant r-2,\text{ and }v_{r-1}=(e_{1}+...+e_{r-1}+e_{r})-r\cdot e_{r},

it is clear that
Y_{0,Q,n}
has a basis

\left\{n_{\alpha}v_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r_{2}\right\}\cup\left\{n_{\alpha,(r)}v_{r-1}\right\}

where
n_{\alpha,(r)}=n_{\alpha}/\gcd(n_{\alpha},r)
, and
Y_{Q,n}
has a basis

\left\{nv_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1\right\}.

It thus follows that

\mathfrak{Q}_{T_{0}}=v_{r-1}(F^{\times n_{\alpha,(r)}})/v_{r-1}(F^{\times n_{\alpha}}).

The action of
g\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
on
\chi_{0}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{T_{0}}}
is given by
\chi_{0}\cdot\chi_{g}
, where

\chi_{g}:\mathfrak{Q}_{T_{0}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

takes the form

\chi_{g}(h)=[g^{-1},h].

We take
g=e_{1}(a),h=v_{r-1}(b^{n_{\alpha,(r)}}),a,b\in F^{\times}
to obtain

(2.14)

\chi_{e_{1}(a)}(v_{r-1}(b^{n_{\alpha,(r)}}))=(b,a)_{n}^{Q(\alpha^{\vee})\cdot n_{\alpha,(r)}}.

Again, there are two special cases:

–

if
\gcd(n_{\alpha},r)=1
, then
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
acts trivially on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{T_{0}}}
.

–

if
\gcd(n_{\alpha},r)=r
(i.e.,
r|n_{\alpha}
), then
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{T_{0}}}
is a torsor over
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
.

We give a partial summary of some results for covers of
{\rm GL}_{r}
, concentrating on the special cases when the induced representation
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{M}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{M}}(\tau\boxtimes\rho)
is irreducible, and when the restriction
\pi|_{\overline{T}_{0}}
is an isotypic sum for
\pi\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{T})
.

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{r}
be a Brylinski–Deligne cover associated with
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathbf{Z}
.

(i)

If
r|n
, then every
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{M}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{M}}(\tau\boxtimes\rho)
is irreducible for
\tau\boxtimes\rho\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{M}^{\dagger})
.

(ii)

If
\gcd(n_{\alpha},r)=1
, then for every
\pi\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{T})
, the restriction
\pi|_{\overline{T}_{0}}
is an isotypic sum. If we assume the stronger equality
\gcd(n,r)=1
, then
\pi|_{\overline{T}^{\dagger}}
is already an isotypic sum.

Proof.

The assertion (i) follows from Theorem
2.11
. The first part of (ii) follows from Corollary
2.12
, while the second part of (ii) is an immediate consequence of second statement (ii) of Theorem
2.11
.
∎

The case of
n
-fold Kazhdan–Patterson covers of
{\rm GL}_{2}
was already discussed extensively in
[
GSS2
, §8]
. For example, (ii) in Proposition
2.16
occurs exactly when one considers restriction of a genuine principal series of odd-degree covers
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{2}
to
\overline{{\rm SL}}_{2}
.

2.4.2.
Covers of
{\rm GSp}_{2r}

Let
{\rm GSp}_{2r}
be the group of similitudes of symplectic type, and let
(X,\Delta,Y,\Delta^{\vee})
be its root data given as follows. The character lattice
X\simeq\mathbf{Z}^{r+1}
has a standard basis

\{e_{i}^{*}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r\}\cup\{e_{0}^{*}\},

where the simple roots are

\Delta=\{e_{i}^{*}-e_{i+1}^{*}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1\}\cup\{2e_{r}^{*}-e_{0}^{*}\}.

The cocharacter lattice
Y\simeq\mathbf{Z}^{r+1}
is given with a basis

\{e_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r\}\cup\{e_{0}\},

such that the paring between
X
and
Y
is
{\left\langle e_{i},e_{j}^{*}\right\rangle}=\delta_{ij}
. The simple coroots are

\Delta^{\vee}=\{e_{i}-e_{i+1}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1\}\cup\{e_{r}\}.

Write

\alpha_{i}=e_{i}^{*}-e_{i+1}^{*},\ \alpha^{\vee}_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}

for
1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1
, and also

\alpha_{r}=2e_{r}^{*}-e_{0}^{*},\ \alpha_{r}^{\vee}=e_{r}.

The Dynkin diagram for the simple coroots is as follows:

We have

Y_{c}=\mathbf{Z}e_{c},\text{ where }e_{c}:=2e_{0}+\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant r}e_{i}.

Consider the covering
\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}
incarnated by
(D,\mathbbm{1})
. The following relations are easy to check:

\displaystyle Q(e_{i})

\displaystyle=-B(e_{i},e_{0})\text{ for every }i;

\displaystyle Q(e_{c})

\displaystyle=B_{Q}(e_{c},e_{0})=4Q(e_{0})-rQ(e_{i})\text{ for any }i.

Here
B(e_{i},e_{0})
dictates the (non-)commutativity of blocks in a covering Levi subgroup of
{\rm GSp}_{2r}
. More precisely, if a Levi subgroup
M\subset{\rm GSp}_{2r}
contains blocks
{\rm GL}_{k}
’s and
{\rm GSp}_{2m}
, then
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{k}
and
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{k^{\prime}}
always commute; however,
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{k}
and
\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2m}
commute if and only if

B(e_{i},e_{0})=0\in\mathbf{Z}/n\mathbf{Z}.

We are interested in those
\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}
whose restriction to
{\rm Sp}_{2r}
is the one with

Q(\alpha_{r}^{\vee})=Q(e_{i})=-1

for every
i
. Thus, we assume

Q(\alpha_{i}^{\vee})=-2\text{ for }1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1,\text{ and }Q(\alpha_{r}^{\vee})=-1.

In this case,

Q(e_{c})=4Q(e_{0})+r.

Since
\Delta^{\vee}\cup\{e_{0}\}
gives a basis for
Y
, to determine
Q
it suffices to specify
Q(e_{0})
. There are two special families of
\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}
(depending on the choice of
Q(e_{0})
) we want to highlight below.

\bullet

(Type I: similitudes-splitting type) If
Q(e_{0})=0
, then the similitude factor
F^{\times}
corresponding to the cocharacter
e_{0}
splits into
\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}
, and we have

\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}\simeq\overline{{\rm Sp}}_{2r}\rtimes F^{\times}.

For
n=2
, this recovers the classical double cover
\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}^{(2)}\simeq\overline{{\rm Sp}}_{2r}^{(2)}\rtimes F^{\times}
as discussed in
[
Szp5
]
. We note that it suffices to take
Q(e_{0})=0\in\mathbf{Z}/n\mathbf{Z}
to ensure the splitting of
\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}
over
e_{0}(F^{\times})
. In any case, if
Q(e_{0})=0
, then

Q(e_{c})=r.

\bullet

(Type II: Kazhdan–Patterson type) The second family of covers of
{\rm GSp}_{2r}
arises from restricting the Kazhdan–Patterson covers of
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{r}
. To avoid confusion, we let

\left\{v_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant 2r\right\}\subset Y

be the standard basis of
{\rm GL}_{2r}
. The one has a natural embedding

{\rm GSp}_{2r}\subset{\rm GL}_{2r}

such that

\alpha_{r}^{\vee}=v_{r}-v_{r+1},\ \alpha_{i}^{\vee}=v_{i}-v_{i+1}-v_{2r+1-i}+v_{2r-i}\text{ and }e_{0}=\sum_{r+1\leqslant i\leqslant 2r}v_{i}.

Let
\overline{{\rm GL}}_{r}
be a Brylinski–Deligne cover associated with
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathbf{Z}
such that
Q(v_{i})=\mathbf{p},B_{Q}(v_{i},v_{j})=\mathbf{q},i\neq j
, as in §
2.4.1
. It is then easy to see that

Q(\alpha_{r}^{\vee})=2\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q},\ Q(\alpha_{i}^{\vee})=2Q(\alpha_{r}^{\vee})\text{ for }1\leqslant i<r

and

Q(e_{0})=r\cdot\frac{(2\mathbf{p}+1)r-1}{2},\quad Q(e_{c})=r\cdot(2(2\mathbf{p}+1)r-1).

In particular, the
n
-fold cover
\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}
obtained from such restriction may not split over the similitude factor
F^{\times}\subset{\rm GSp}_{2r}
.

We will carry out the computation mainly for type (I) and (II) covers of
{\rm GSp}_{2r}
as above.

Let
{\bf r}=(r_{1},r_{2},...,r_{k},m)
be an ordered partition of
r
. Let

M_{\bf r}={\rm GL}_{r_{1}}\times...\times{\rm GL}_{r_{k}}\times{\rm GSp}_{2m}\subset{\rm GSp}_{2r}

be the associated Levi subgroup. We have
G_{0}={\rm Sp}_{2r}
, and

M_{0}={\rm GL}_{r_{1}}\times...\times{\rm GL}_{r_{k}}\times{\rm Sp}_{2m}\subset{\rm Sp}_{2r}

is the Levi subgroup of
{\rm Sp}_{2r}
. Every element in
T\subset{\rm GSp}_{2r}
is of the form

(a;\lambda):=(a_{1},...,a_{r};\lambda):=\Big(\prod_{i=1}^{r}e_{i}(a_{i})\Big)\cdot e_{0}(\lambda)\text{ with }a_{i},\lambda\in F^{\times}.

Here
(a;\lambda)\in T_{0}
if and only if
\lambda=1
.

Lemma 2.17
.

For
(a;\lambda),(b;\delta)\in T
, one has the commutator

[(a;\lambda),(b;\delta)]=(\lambda,\det(b))_{n}^{-Q(\alpha_{r}^{\vee})}\cdot(\det(a),\delta)_{n}^{-Q(\alpha^{\vee}_{r})}\cdot(\lambda,\delta)_{n}^{2Q(e_{0})}\cdot\prod_{i}(a_{i},b_{i})_{n}^{2Q(\alpha_{r}^{\vee})}.

Proof.

By abuse of notation, we will write
[a,b]
for
[(a;1),(b;1)]
, and
[\lambda,b]
for
[(1;\lambda),(b;1)]
. One has

[(a;\lambda),(b;\delta)]=[\lambda,b]\cdot[a,b]\cdot[\lambda,\delta]\cdot[a,\delta]\in\mu_{n}.

Now, it is easy to obtain

\displaystyle[\lambda,b]

\displaystyle=(\lambda,\det(b))_{n}^{B(e_{0},e_{1})}=(\lambda,\det(b))_{n}^{-Q(\alpha_{r}^{\vee})},

\displaystyle[a,b]

\displaystyle=\prod_{i}(a_{i},b_{i})_{n}^{2Q(e_{i})}=\prod_{i}(a_{i},b_{i})_{n}^{2Q(\alpha_{r}^{\vee})},

\displaystyle[\lambda,\delta]

\displaystyle=(\lambda,\delta)_{n}^{2Q(e_{0})},

\displaystyle[a,\delta]

\displaystyle=(\det(a),\delta)_{n}^{B(e_{1},e_{0})}=(\det(a),\delta)_{n}^{-Q(\alpha^{\vee}_{r})}.

The result immediately follows from combining these equalities.
∎

For
\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}
of type (I) or (II), we have
Q(\alpha_{r}^{\vee})=-1
and thus

[(a;\lambda),(b;\delta)]=(\lambda,\det(b))_{n}\cdot(\det(a),\delta)_{n}\cdot(\lambda,\delta)_{n}^{2Q(e_{0})}\cdot\prod_{i}(a_{i},b_{i})_{n}^{-2}.

Now, we want to explicate the three groups
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger},\mathfrak{Q}_{G}
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}
. Equivalently, we want to determine the sublattices of
Y
associated with the following groups

\overline{T}^{\dagger},\ Z(\overline{Z(G)}),\ Z(\overline{G}),\ Z(\overline{M}_{0})\text{ and }\overline{M}_{0}\cap Z(\overline{M}).

As
\mathbbm{L}(\overline{T}^{\dagger})=Y_{c}+Y_{0}
, we see that

\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}\simeq e_{0}(F^{\times})/e_{0}(F^{\times 2}),

where representatives are taken from
e_{0}(F^{\times})
. We have

\mathbbm{L}(Z(\overline{Z(G)}))=Y_{c,Q,n}=\frac{\mathbf{Z}\cdot ne_{c}}{\gcd(n,2Q(e_{c}))}=:\mathbf{Z}(n_{1}e_{c})

and

\mathbbm{L}(Z(\overline{G}))=Y_{c}\cap Y_{Q,n}=\frac{\mathbf{Z}\cdot ne_{c}}{\gcd(n,Q(e_{c}))}=:\mathbf{Z}(n_{2}e_{c}).

Thus,

\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}=e_{c}(F^{\times n_{1}})/e_{c}(F^{\times n_{2}}).

For every natural number
n
, we let

\wp(n)\in\mathbf{N}_{\geqslant 0}

be the 2-exponent in
n
such that

n=2^{\wp(n)}\cdot n^{\prime}

with
n^{\prime}
odd.

Lemma 2.18
.

For
\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}
of type (I) or (II), we have:

(i)

if
\wp(n)\leqslant\wp(r)
, then
\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}=\left\{1\right\}
;

(ii)

if
\wp(n)>\wp(r)
, then
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
is a torsor over
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

One has

Q(e_{c})=\begin{cases}r&\text{ if $\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}$ is of type (I) },\\
r\cdot(2(2\mathbf{p}+1)r-1)&\text{ if $\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}$ is of type (II)}.\end{cases}

If
\wp(n)\leqslant\wp(r)
, the assertion is clear. Now we assume
\wp(n)>\wp(r)
. The action of
e_{0}(a)\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
is given by the multiplication of the character

\chi_{e_{0}(a)}:\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

given by

\chi_{e_{0}(a)}(e_{c}(b^{n_{1}}))=(b,a)_{n}^{n_{1}\cdot B(e_{0},e_{c})}=(b,a)_{n}^{n_{1}\cdot Q(e_{c})}.

If
\chi_{e_{0}(a)}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
is the trivial character, then
a\in F^{\times 2}
, i.e.,
e_{0}(a)=1\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
. That is,
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
acts freely on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
. Since in this case
n_{2}=2n_{1}
, the two groups
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}\simeq F^{\times}/F^{\times 2}
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}\simeq F^{\times n_{1}}/F^{\times n_{2}}
have the same size. Hence,
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
is a torsor over
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
.
∎

To determine
Z(\overline{M}_{0})
, we first note that for every
I\subset(\mathbf{N}\cap[1,r])
, one has

Q\Big(\sum_{i\in I}e_{i}\Big)=\left|I\right|.

Let
e_{c,i}\in Y
be such that
Y_{{\rm GL}_{r_{i}},c}=\mathbf{Z}e_{c,i},1\leqslant i\leqslant k
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbbm{L}(Z(\overline{M}_{0}))

\displaystyle=Y_{M_{0},c}\cap Y_{0,Q,n}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\mathbf{Z}\cdot ne_{c,i}}{\gcd(n,2Q(e_{i}))}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k}\frac{\mathbf{Z}\cdot ne_{c,i}}{\gcd(n,Q(\alpha_{1}^{\vee}))}

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{k}y_{i}e_{c,i}:n_{\alpha_{1}}|(y_{i})\text{ for every }i\right\}.

It is not hard to see that

\mathbbm{L}(\overline{M}_{0}\cap Z(\overline{M}))=Y_{M_{0},c}\cap Y_{Q,n}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\sum_{i=1}^{k}y_{i}e_{c,i}\in Y_{M_{0},c}:\\
\bullet\quad n_{\alpha_{1}}|y_{i}\text{ for every }i,\\
\bullet\quad n_{\alpha_{r}}|(\sum_{i=1}^{k}y_{i}r_{i}).\end{array}\right\}.

Lemma 2.19
.

Let
M\subset{\rm GSp}_{2r}
be the Levi subgroup associated with the partition
{\bf r}=(r_{1},r_{2},...,r_{k};m)
of
r
. There are three cases:

(i)

if
n_{\alpha_{1}}=n_{\alpha_{r}}
, then
\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}=\left\{1\right\}
;

(ii)

if
n_{\alpha_{r}}=2\cdot n_{\alpha_{1}}
and
r_{i}
is even for every
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
, then
\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}=\left\{1\right\}
;

(iii)

if
n_{\alpha_{r}}=2\cdot n_{\alpha_{1}}
and
r_{i}
is odd for some
i
, then
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}
is a torsor over
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

First, (i) and (ii) are clear since in this case

n_{\alpha_{r}}|(\sum_{i=1}^{k}y_{i}r_{i})

holds whenever
n_{\alpha_{1}}|y_{i}
for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
.
Now for (iii), the assumption implies that
\mathbbm{L}(\overline{M}_{0}\cap Z(\overline{M}))
is of index two inside
\mathbbm{L}(Z(\overline{M}_{0}))
, and representatives of
\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}
can be chosen from
e_{c,i}(b^{n_{\alpha_{1}}}),b\in F^{\times}
. The action of
e_{0}(a)\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}
is given by the multiplication with the character

\chi_{e_{0}(a)}:\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

which takes the form

\chi_{e_{0}(a)(e_{c,i}(b^{n_{\alpha_{1}}}))}=(b,a)_{n}^{r_{i}n_{\alpha_{1}}\cdot B(e_{0},e_{i})}=(b,a)_{n}^{-r_{i}n_{\alpha_{1}}Q(\alpha_{r}^{\vee})}.

If
\chi_{e_{0}(a)}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}
is trivial, then
a\in F^{\times 2}
, i.e.,
e_{0}(a)=1\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
. On the other hand,
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}
have the same size. Hence,
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}}
is a torsor over
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
.
∎

For
n=2
, this agrees with the results in
[
Szp4-1
]
, as expected. Note that if
(a_{1},a_{2},...,a_{k})
is a partition of an odd
a\in\mathbf{N}
, then one of the
a_{j}
’s is odd. It follows that if the assertion (iii) in Lemma
2.19
holds for
M
, then it holds for every Levi subgroup
M^{\prime}\subset M\subset G
. This is consistent with Corollary
2.13
.

Similar as Proposition
2.16
, we give a partial summary for the above discussion on
\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}
. Combining Theorem
2.11
, Lemma
2.18
and Lemma
2.19
, we have the following.

Proposition 2.20
.

Let
\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r}
be a cover of
{\rm GSp}_{2r}
either of type (I) or (II).

(i)

For a Levi subgroup
M
associated with
(r_{1},...,r_{k},m)
, if

–

either
\wp(n)>\wp(r)
, or

–

n
is even and
r_{i}
is odd for some
i
,

then
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{M}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{M}}(\tau\boxtimes\rho)
is irreducible for every
\tau\boxtimes\rho\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{M}^{\dagger})
.

(ii)

If
n
is odd, then for every
\pi\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{T})
, its restriction to
\overline{T}^{\dagger}
is an isotypic sum; hence the restriction
\pi|_{\overline{T}_{0}}
is also an isotypic sum.

2.4.3.
Covers of
{\rm GSpin}_{2r+1}

We consider the simply-connected algebraic group
{\rm Spin}_{2r+1}
which sits in the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Spin}_{2r+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm SO}_{2r+1},}

where
Z=\left\{1,z\right\}
is the center of
{\rm Spin}_{2r+1}
.
The Dynkin diagram for the simple coroots of the group
{\rm Spin}_{2r+1}
is as follows:

Pushing out the above exact sequence by the embedding
Z\hookrightarrow{\rm GL}_{1}
with
z
sent to
-1
, one obtains an exact sequence (see
[
Asg02
]
)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm GL}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm GSpin}_{2r+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm SO}_{2r+1},}

where by definition

{\rm GSpin}_{2r+1}=\frac{{\rm GL}_{1}\times{\rm Spin}_{2r+1}}{\left\{(1,1),(-1,z)\right\}}.

In
[
ASh1
]
, the group
{\rm GSpin}_{2r}
is also discussed. One of the advantegeous properties of
{\rm GSpin}_{2r+1}
is that its Levi subgroups are associated with partitions
(r_{1},...,r_{k},m)
of
r
and take the form of
{\rm GL}_{r_{1}}\times...\times{\rm GL}_{r_{k}}\times{\rm GSpin}_{2m+1}
; similarly for
{\rm GSpin}_{2r}
. However, the Levi subgroups of
{\rm Spin}_{2r+1}
are complicated to describe explicitly, as explained in
[
Asg02
]
and discussed with details in
[
Mat09
]
.

The Langlands dual group of
{\rm GSpin}_{2r+1}
is
{\rm GSp}_{2r}
, and thus the root datum

(X,\Delta,Y,\Delta^{\vee})

of the former is obtained from inverting that of the latter, which is given in §
2.4.2
. The character group
X\simeq\mathbf{Z}^{r+1}
is given with a basis

\{e_{i}^{*}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r\}\cup\{e_{0}^{*}\}.

The simple roots are

\Delta=\{e_{i}^{*}-e_{i+1}^{*}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1\}\cup\{e_{r}^{*}\}.

The cocharacter group
Y\simeq\mathbf{Z}^{r+1}
has a standard basis

\{e_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r\}\cup\{e_{0}\},

where the simple coroots are

\Delta^{\vee}=\{e_{i}-e_{i+1}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1\}\cup\{2e_{r}-e_{0}\}.

Write

\alpha_{i}=e_{i}^{*}-e_{i+1}^{*},\quad\alpha^{\vee}_{i}=e_{i}-e_{i+1}

for
1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1
, and also

\alpha_{r}=e_{r}^{*},\quad\alpha_{r}^{\vee}=2e_{r}-e_{0}.

Consider the covering
\overline{{\rm GSpin}}_{2r+1}
incarnated by
(D,\mathbbm{1})
. We are interested in those
\overline{{\rm GSpin}}_{2r}
whose restriction to
{\rm Spin}_{2r+1}
is the one with
Q(\alpha_{1}^{\vee})=1
. That is, we assume

Q(\alpha_{i}^{\vee})=1\text{ for }1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1,\text{ and }Q(\alpha_{r}^{\vee})=2.

We have

Y_{c}=\mathbf{Z}e_{0}

and thus

0=B_{Q}(\alpha_{r}^{\vee},e_{0})=2B_{Q}(e_{r},e_{0})-2Q(e_{0}).

Hence,

(2.15)

B_{Q}(e_{i},e_{0})=Q(e_{0})

for every
i
. It also follows from the two equalities

Q(e_{i})=Q(e_{j})\text{ for every }i,j\text{ and }Q(2e_{r}-e_{0})=2Q(e_{i}-e_{i+1})

that

(2.16)

Q(e_{0})=2B(e_{i},e_{j})\text{ for every }i\neq j.

On the other hand, we have

Q(\alpha_{i}^{\vee})=2Q(e_{i})-B(e_{i},e_{i+1})

for
1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1
. Thus, the cover
\overline{{\rm GSp}}_{2r+1}
we are interested in is determined by the two numbers

\mathbf{p}:=Q(e_{i})\text{ and }\mathbf{q}:=B(e_{i},e_{j})\text{ for }i\neq j

constrained by

2\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}=1.

Again, we want to determine the three groups
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger},\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}
and the structure of the Pontryagin duals of latter two under the action of the first. For
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}
, we want to determine the sublattices of
Y
associated with the following groups

\overline{T}^{\dagger},\ Z(\overline{Z(G)}),\ Z(\overline{G}).

To avoid technical difficulties while dealing with general
M_{0}
(see
[
Mat09
]
), for
\mathfrak{Q}_{M_{0}}
we only illustrate the minimal parabolic case when
\overline{M}=\overline{T}
, and thus consider

\ Z(\overline{T}_{0})\text{ and }\overline{T}_{0}\cap Z(\overline{T}).

First,
\mathbbm{L}(\overline{T}^{\dagger})=Y_{c}+Y_{0}
with

Y/(Y_{c}+Y_{0})=\left\{0,e_{r}\right\}.

Hence,

\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}\simeq e_{r}(F^{\times})/e_{r}(F^{\times 2}),

where representatives are taken from
e_{r}(F^{\times})
. We have

\mathbbm{L}(Z(\overline{Z(G)}))=Y_{c,Q,n}=\frac{\mathbf{Z}\cdot ne_{0}}{\gcd(n,2Q(e_{0}))}=:\mathbf{Z}(n_{1}e_{0})

and

\mathbbm{L}(Z(\overline{G}))=Y_{c}\cap Y_{Q,n}=\frac{\mathbf{Z}\cdot ne_{0}}{\gcd(n,Q(e_{0}))}=:\mathbf{Z}(n_{2}e_{0}).

This gives that

\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}=e_{0}(F^{\times n_{1}})/e_{0}(F^{\times n_{2}}).

Lemma 2.21
.

We have two cases for the action of
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
:

(i)

if
\wp(n_{1})=0
, or equivalently
n_{2}=n_{1}
, then
\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}=\left\{1\right\}
;

(ii)

if
\wp(n_{1})\geqslant 1
, or equivalently
n_{2}=2n_{1}
, then
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
is a torsor over
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

The assertion (i) is clear. For (ii), we have
\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}\simeq F^{\times n_{1}}/F^{\times 2n_{1}}
, which has the same size as
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}\simeq F^{\times}/F^{\times 2}
. It suffices to show that the action of
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
is free. Consider the character
\chi_{e_{r}(a)}\in\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
given by

\chi_{e_{r}(a)}(e_{0}(b^{n_{1}}))=(b,a)_{n}^{n_{1}\cdot B(e_{r},e_{0})}=(b,a)_{n}^{n_{1}\cdot Q(e_{0})}.

If
\chi_{e_{r}(a)}
is the trivial character of
\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}
, then
a\in F^{\times 2}
, i.e.,
e_{r}(a)=1\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
. This shows that the action of
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{Z}}
is free and completes the proof.
∎

To compute
\mathfrak{Q}_{T_{0}}
, we have

\mathbbm{L}(Z(\overline{T}_{0}))=Y_{0,Q,n}

and

\mathbbm{L}(\overline{T}_{0}\cap Z(\overline{T}))=Y_{0}\cap Y_{Q,n}.

A simple computation gives that

Y_{0}\cap Y_{Q,n}=Y_{Q,n}^{sc}=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathbf{Z}(n_{\alpha_{i}}\alpha_{i}^{\vee}),

where
n_{\alpha_{i}}=n/\gcd(n,Q(\alpha_{i}^{\vee}))
. Note that this shows that the cover
\overline{{\rm GSpin}}_{2r+1}
is always saturated in the sense of
[
Ga6
, Definition 2.1]
. Regarding the lattice
Y_{0,Q,n}
, we have two cases:

–

if
n
is odd or
4|(2r-n)
, then
Y_{0,Q,n}=Y_{Q,n}^{sc}
, and we have the dual group

\overline{{\rm Spin}}_{2r+1}^{\vee}=\begin{cases}{\rm PGSp}_{2r}&\text{ if $n$ is odd},\\
{\rm SO}_{2r+1}&\text{ if $4|(2r-n)$}.\end{cases}

–

if
4\nmid(2r-n)
with
n=2m
, for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant r
defining

v_{i}:=m(2e_{i}-e_{0})\text{ and }v_{0}:=\frac{v_{1}+v_{2}+...+v_{r}}{2},

then one can check easily that

\left\{v_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant r-1\right\}\cup\left\{v_{0}\right\}

constitutes a basis of
Y_{0,Q,n}
. In this case,

\overline{{\rm Spin}}_{2r+1}^{\vee}={\rm Spin}_{2r+1}.

It is clear that in the first case above (
n
is odd or
4|(2r-n)
), one has

\mathfrak{Q}_{T_{0}}=\left\{1\right\}.

On the other hand, if
4\nmid(2r-n)
, then

\mathfrak{Q}_{T_{0}}=v_{0}(F^{\times})/v_{0}(F^{\times 2})\simeq F^{\times}/F^{\times 2},

where more explicitly
v_{0}=m(e_{1}+e_{2}+...+e_{r})-(mr\cdot e_{0})/2
.
The action of
e_{r}(a)\in\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
on
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{T_{0}}}
is given multiplication by the character

\chi_{e_{r}(a)}:\mathfrak{Q}_{T_{0}}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times},

which takes the form

\chi_{e_{r}(a)}(v_{0}(b))=(b,a)_{n}^{B_{Q}(e_{r},v_{0})}=(b,a)_{n}^{mQ(\alpha_{1}^{\vee})}=(b,a)_{2}.

From this it follows that
\widehat{\mathfrak{Q}_{T_{0}}}
is a
\mathfrak{Q}^{\dagger}
-torsor in this case.

We give a summary of the results for
{\rm GSpin}_{2r+1}
below.

Proposition 2.22
.

Let
\overline{{\rm GSpin}}_{2r+1}
be a Brylinski–Deligne cover associated with a quadratic form
Q
such that
Q(\alpha_{r}^{\vee})=2
.

(i)

If
\wp(n)\geqslant 3
, then
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{M}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{M}}\tau\boxtimes\rho
is irreducible for every
\tau\boxtimes\rho\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{M}^{\dagger})
. Moreover, if
4\nmid(2r-n)
with
n
even, then
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{T}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{T}}\tau\boxtimes\rho
is irreducible for
\tau\boxtimes\rho\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{T}^{\dagger})
.

(ii)

If
n
is odd or
4|(2r-n)
, then the restriction of every
\pi\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{T})
to
\overline{T}_{0}
is an isotypic sum. Moreover, if
\wp(n)=2
and
2|r
, then the restriction
\pi|_{\overline{T}^{\dagger}}
is already an isotypic sum.

2.5.
Restriction of genuine principal series

In this subsection, we consider exclusively the restriction of genuine principal series (especially in the tame case) by using a direct analysis from the perspective of Mackey’s theory.

The notations are the same as before,
\overline{G}
is a cover of
G
and
\overline{G}_{0}=\overline{G}_{\rm der}
the derived subgroup of
\overline{G}
. Let
\overline{T}_{0}\subset\overline{G}_{0}
be the covering torus.

Every genuine principal series is parabolically induced from an irreducible representation of
\overline{T}
of the form

{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}}^{\overline{T}}(\tilde{\chi})

where
\overline{A}\subset\overline{T}
is a maximal abelian subgroup, necessarily containing
Z(\overline{T})
, and

\tilde{\chi}:\ \overline{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

is an extension of the central character

\chi:Z(\overline{T})\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}.

For every
\gamma\in\overline{T}_{0}\backslash\overline{T}/\overline{A}
, denote

\overline{A}_{\gamma}=(\gamma\overline{A}\gamma^{-1})\cap\overline{T}_{0}.

Define the character

{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}:\overline{A}_{\gamma}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

by

{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}(g)=\tilde{\chi}(\gamma^{-1}g\gamma)\text{ for }g\in\overline{A}_{\gamma}.

The Mackey theory gives a decomposition

\left({\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}}^{\overline{T}}(\tilde{\chi})\right)|_{\overline{T}_{0}}=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\overline{T}_{0}\backslash\overline{T}/\overline{A}}{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}_{\gamma}}^{\overline{T}_{0}}({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}).

We have the following inclusions between various groups

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{A}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{T}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{A}_{\gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\overline{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\overline{T})\cap\overline{T}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\overline{T}_{0}).}

Here the dashed arrow indicates that there may not be an inclusion
Z(\overline{T}_{0})\subset\overline{A}_{\gamma}
; in particular,
\overline{A}_{\gamma}
may not be a maximal abelian subgroup of
\overline{T}_{0}
.

Definition 2.23
.

A pair
(\overline{G},\overline{G}_{0})
is called an isotypic-pair if any of the following equivalent conditions (see Corollary
2.12
) is satisfied:

(i)

the equality
Y_{0}\cap Y_{Q,n}=Y_{0,Q,n}
holds;

(ii)

the inclusion
Z(\overline{T}_{0})\subset Z(\overline{T})
holds.

To continue the analysis, we specialize to the tame case when
p\nmid n
. In this case, we fix a splitting of
\overline{G}
over
K:={\bf G}(O)
denoted by

s_{K}:\ K\longrightarrow\overline{G}.

This gives inherited splittings of
{\bf G}_{0}(O),{\bf T}(O)
and
{\bf T}_{0}(O)
in the respective covering groups. If no confusion arises, we will omit
s_{K}
and simply write
K\subset\overline{G}
. The group

\overline{A}=Z(\overline{T})\cdot{\bf T}(O)

is a maximal abelian subgroup of
\overline{T}
. One has

\overline{A}_{\gamma}=(Z(\overline{T})\cap\overline{T}_{0})\cdot({}^{\gamma}{\bf T}(O)\cap\overline{T}_{0})=(Z(\overline{T})\cap\overline{T}_{0})\cdot{}^{\gamma}{\bf T}_{0}(O)\subset Z(\overline{T}_{0})\cdot{\bf T}_{0}(O),

where
{}^{\gamma}{\bf T}_{0}(O)=\gamma{\bf T}_{0}(O)\gamma^{-1}
for every
\gamma
. Note that

\overline{A}_{0}:=Z(\overline{T}_{0})\cdot{\bf T}_{0}(O)

is a maximal abelian subgroup of
\overline{T}_{0}
. Defining

\overline{A}^{\natural}=(Z(\overline{T})\cap\overline{T}_{0})\cdot{\bf T}_{0}(O),

We write every

\tilde{\chi}:\overline{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

as

\tilde{\chi}=\chi\boxtimes\tilde{\chi}_{O},

where
\chi
is the central character mentioned above, and

\tilde{\chi}_{O}:{\bf T}(O)\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

is a character such that
\chi
and
\tilde{\chi}_{O}
agree on the intersection
Z(\overline{T})\cap{\bf T}(O)
.
Thus the character
{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}
of
\overline{A}_{\gamma}
is just

{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}=({}^{\gamma}\chi)\boxtimes({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}_{O})=\chi\boxtimes({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}_{O}),

where

{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}_{O}:{}^{\gamma}{\bf T}(O)\cap\overline{T}_{0}={}^{\gamma}{\bf T}_{0}(O)\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

is given by

{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}_{O}({}^{\gamma}k_{0})=\tilde{\chi}_{O}(k_{0})

for every
k_{0}\in{\bf T}_{0}(O)
. In view of the identification

\overline{A}_{\gamma}=\overline{A}^{\natural}\text{ given by }z\cdot{}^{\gamma}k_{0}\mapsto(z\cdot[\gamma,k_{0}])\cdot k_{0},

where
z\in Z(\overline{T})\cap\overline{T}_{0}
and
k_{0}\in{\bf T}_{0}(O)
, we can identify

{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}=\chi\boxtimes{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}_{O}:\overline{A}_{\gamma}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

with a character

{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}=\chi\boxtimes([\gamma,-]^{-1}\tilde{\chi}_{O}):\overline{A}^{\natural}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}.

Thus, we have

\left({\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}}^{\overline{T}}(\tilde{\chi})\right)|_{\overline{T}_{0}}=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\overline{T}/(\overline{T}_{0}\overline{A})}{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}^{\natural}}^{\overline{T}_{0}}({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}),

where

(2.17)

\overline{T}/(\overline{T}_{0}\overline{A})\simeq Y/(Y_{0}+Y_{Q,n})=:\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\Gamma}.

Now we analyze the decomposition of
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}^{\natural}}^{\overline{T}_{0}}({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi})
. By induction in stages, one has

{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}^{\natural}}^{\overline{T}_{0}}({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi})={\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}_{0}}^{\overline{T}_{0}}\ {\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}^{\natural}}^{\overline{A}_{0}}({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}).

Denote by

\mathscr{E}({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi};\overline{A}^{\natural},\overline{A}_{0})

the set of all the possible extensions of
{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}
to
\overline{A}_{0}
; then it is exactly the set of irreducible constituents of
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}^{\natural}}^{\overline{A}_{0}}({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi})
. We thus have

{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}^{\natural}}^{\overline{T}_{0}}({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi})=\bigoplus_{({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi})^{\prime}\in\mathscr{E}({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi};\overline{A}^{\natural},\overline{A}_{0})}{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}_{0}}^{\overline{T}_{0}}(({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi})^{\prime}).

Recall that
\overline{T}_{0}
is a Heisenberg-type group and thus irreducible genuine representation is determined by the central character. In particular, the isomorphism class of
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}_{0}}^{\overline{T}_{0}}(({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi})^{\prime})
is in fact determined by the restriction of
({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi})^{\prime}
to
Z(\overline{T}_{0})
. We thus consider

\mathscr{E}(\chi,{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}_{O};Z(\overline{T}_{0}))=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\omega_{\gamma,j}:Z(\overline{T}_{0})\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}:\\
\bullet\quad\omega_{\gamma,j}\text{ extends }\chi|_{Z(\overline{T})\cap\overline{T}_{0}},\\
\bullet\quad\omega_{\gamma,j}\text{ extends }([\gamma,\cdot]^{-1}\tilde{\chi}_{O})|_{{\bf T}_{0}(O)\cap Z(\overline{T}_{0})}.\end{array}\right\}.

We note that
\chi
and
[\gamma,\cdot]^{-1}\tilde{\chi}_{O}
are compatible on the intersection of
Z(\overline{T})\cap\overline{T}_{0}
and
{\bf T}_{0}(O)\cap Z(\overline{T}_{0})
.
It is easy to see that there is a natural bijection

\mathscr{E}(\chi,{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}_{O};Z(\overline{T}_{0}))\longrightarrow\mathscr{E}({}^{\gamma}\chi;\overline{A}^{\natural},\overline{A}_{0})

with size

\left|\mathscr{E}(\chi,{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}_{O};Z(\overline{T}_{0}))\right|=\left|Y_{0,Q,n}/(Y_{0}\cap Y_{Q,n})\right|.

This gives the bounds for the index
j
in
\omega_{\gamma,j}
as

1\leqslant j\leqslant\left|Y_{0,Q,n}/(Y_{0}\cap Y_{Q,n})\right|.

We also have

(2.18)

{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}^{\natural}}^{\overline{T}_{0}}({}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi})=\bigoplus_{\omega_{\gamma,j}\in\mathscr{E}(\chi,{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}_{O};Z(\overline{T}_{0}))}{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}_{0}}^{\overline{T}_{0}}(\tilde{\omega}_{\gamma,j})=\bigoplus_{\omega_{\gamma,j}\in\mathscr{E}(\chi,{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}_{O};Z(\overline{T}_{0}))}i(\omega_{\gamma,j}),

where in the middle term

\tilde{\omega}_{\gamma,j}:\overline{A}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

is any extension of
\omega_{\gamma,j}
to
\overline{A}_{0}
. Here
i(\omega_{\gamma,j})
denotes the isomorphism class of
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}_{0}}^{\overline{T}_{0}}(\tilde{\omega}_{\gamma,j})
, which only depends on the central character
\omega_{\gamma,j}
. Note that the decomposition in (
2.18
) is multiplicity-free.
We thus get

(2.19)

\left({\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}}^{\overline{T}}(\tilde{\chi})\right)|_{\overline{T}_{0}}=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\Gamma}}\bigoplus_{\omega_{\gamma,j}\in\mathscr{E}(\chi,{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}_{O};Z(\overline{T}_{0}))}i(\omega_{\gamma,j}),

where we recall that

\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\Gamma}=Y/(Y_{0}+Y_{Q,n})

by definition in (
2.17
).

To simplify the formula (
2.19
), we consider the group homomorphism

(2.20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{c}:\ \mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\Gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Hom}({\bf T}_{0}(O)\cap Z(\overline{T}_{0}),\mu_{n})}

given by

\mathfrak{c}(y)(k_{0}):=[y(\varpi),k_{0}]\in\mu_{n}.

Define

Y^{\mathfrak{c}}=\left\{y\in Y:B_{Q}(y,y^{\prime})\in n\mathbf{Z}\text{ for all }y^{\prime}\in Y_{0,Q,n}\right\}\subset Y.

Note that
{\bf T}_{0}(O)\cap Z(\overline{T}_{0})=Y_{0,Q,n}(O)
, and thus

\mathfrak{c}(y)(y^{\prime}(u))=(\varpi,u)_{n}^{B_{Q}(y,y^{\prime})}

for every
y^{\prime}\in Y_{0,Q,n}
. Hence,

\mathfrak{c}(y)=\mathbbm{1}

if and only if
y\in Y^{\mathfrak{c}}
.
It is easy to see that

Y_{0}+Y_{Q,n}\subset Y^{\mathfrak{c}}

and thus we have the well-defined group

(2.21)

\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\mathfrak{c}}:=Y^{\mathfrak{c}}/(Y_{Q,n}+Y_{0})\subset\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\Gamma}.

We summarize the above discussion to give the following:

Theorem 2.24
.

Assume
p\nmid n
. Let
i(\chi)={\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}}^{\overline{T}}(\tilde{\chi})\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{T})
be with central character
\chi
. Let
I(\chi)
be the associated genuine principal series of
\overline{G}
.

(i)

One has
{\rm Ker}(\mathfrak{c})=\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\mathfrak{c}}
and thus

i(\chi)|_{\overline{T}_{0}}=\left|\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\mathfrak{c}}\right|\cdot\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\Gamma}/\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\mathfrak{c}}}\bigoplus_{\omega_{\gamma,j}\in\mathscr{E}(\chi,{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}_{O};Z(\overline{T}_{0}))}i(\omega_{\gamma,j}),

where
i(\omega_{\gamma,j})
appears with multiplicity one in the double sum of the right hand side.

(ii)

Consequently,

I(\chi)|_{\overline{G}_{0}}=\left|\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\mathfrak{c}}\right|\cdot\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\Gamma}/\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\mathfrak{c}}}\bigoplus_{\omega_{\gamma,j}\in\mathscr{E}(\chi,{}^{\gamma}\tilde{\chi}_{O};Z(\overline{T}_{0}))}I(\omega_{\gamma,j})

for the restriction of genuine principal series
I(\chi)
. Here generically, the
I(\omega_{\gamma,j})
’s appear multiplicity-free in the above decompostion.

(iii)

Assume that
I(\chi)
is a
(K,s_{K})
-unramified genuine principal series. Then
I(\omega_{\gamma,j})
is
(K_{0},s_{K})
-unramified if and only if
\omega_{\gamma,j}
belongs to the set
\mathscr{E}(\chi,\tilde{\chi}_{O};Z(\overline{T}_{0}))
, i.e., with
\gamma=0\in\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\Gamma}/\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\mathfrak{c}}
being the trivial class.

Corollary 2.25
.

Assume
p\nmid n
. If
(\overline{G},\overline{G}_{0})
is an isotypic-pair, then

\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\Gamma}=\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\mathfrak{c}};

in this case

i(\chi)|_{\overline{T}_{0}}=i(\omega)^{\oplus\left|\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\Gamma}\right|}\text{ and }I(\chi)|_{\overline{G}_{0}}=I(\omega)^{\oplus\left|\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\Gamma}\right|},

are both isotypic sums with multiplicity
\left|\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\Gamma}\right|
, where
\omega=\chi|_{Z(\overline{T}_{0})}
is the unique character in
\mathscr{E}(\chi,\tilde{\chi}_{O};Z(\overline{T}_{0}))
.

Proof.

Under the assumption, we have
Y^{\mathfrak{c}}=Y
and thus
\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\mathfrak{c}}=\mathscr{X}_{Q,n}^{\Gamma}
.
The rest is clear in view of Theorem
2.24
.
∎

Remark 2.26
.

If
I(\chi)
is
(K,s_{K})
-unramified, then it is
(\gamma\cdot K,\gamma\cdot s_{K})
-unramified as well. Here
\gamma\cdot K=\gamma K\gamma^{-1}
and
\gamma\cdot s_{K}
is a splitting of
\overline{G}
over
\gamma\cdot K
given by

(\gamma\cdot s_{K})(\gamma\cdot k):=\gamma\cdot s_{K}(k)\cdot\gamma^{-1}.

Then every
I(\omega_{\gamma,j})
is in fact
(\gamma\cdot K_{0},\gamma\cdot s_{K})
-unramified. Note that
\gamma\cdot K_{0}
and
K_{0}
may not be in the same
G_{0}
-conjugacy classes of maximal compact subgroup of
G_{0}
. See §
5.2
for a general discussion of this, and the reader may also refer to §
5.4
and §
6
for examples.

3.
L-parameters and functoriality

We continue to assume in this section that
Z({\bf G})
is connected as in §
2.3
.

3.1.
Relations among several L-groups

Recall that from Lemma
2.7
the group
Z(\overline{G})
is associated with the sublattice

Y_{z}:=Y_{Q,n}\cap Y_{c}.

We have the L-group extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\overline{G})^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}Z(\overline{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{WD}_{F}}

where
Z(\overline{G})^{\vee}:={\rm Hom}(Y_{z},\mathbf{C}^{\times})
denotes the dual group of
Z(\overline{G})
.
Similarly, we have an L-group for
\overline{Z(G)}
as an extension

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{Z(G)}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{Z(G)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{WD}_{F}.}

Here
\overline{Z(G)}^{\vee}:={\rm Hom}(Y_{c,Q,n},\mathbf{C}^{\times})
. It is easy to see that

(3.1)

Y_{z}\subset Y_{c,Q,n}

and thus there is a natural homormorphism

(3.2)

f_{c,z}:{}^{L}\overline{Z(G)}\longrightarrow{}^{L}Z(\overline{G})

such that

f_{c,z}|_{\overline{Z(G)}^{\vee}}:\overline{Z(G)}^{\vee}\longrightarrow Z(\overline{G})^{\vee}

is just the restriction map

{\rm Hom}(Y_{c,Q,n},\mathbf{C}^{\times})\longrightarrow{\rm Hom}(Y_{z},\mathbf{C}^{\times})

induced from (
3.1
).

On the other hand, the dual group for
\overline{G}
has root datum
(Y_{Q,n},\Phi_{Q,n}^{\vee};X_{Q,n},\Phi_{Q,n})
, where
Y_{Q,n}
is the character lattice for
\overline{G}^{\vee}
. We see that there is a natural homomorphism

f_{G,z}:\overline{G}^{\vee}\longrightarrow Z(\overline{G})^{\vee}

which extends to a homomorphism of L-groups

(3.3)

f_{G,z}:{}^{L}\overline{G}\longrightarrow{}^{L}Z(\overline{G}),

depending on the choice of a distinguished character
\chi_{\psi}
. We give some details of the map
f_{G,z}
as follows. Let
{\bf G}_{Q,n}
be the split linear algebraic group over
F
such that the Langlands dual group of
G_{Q,n}={\bf G}_{Q,n}(F)
is equal to
\overline{G}^{\vee}
, i.e.,

G_{Q,n}^{\vee}=\overline{G}^{\vee}.

One can restrict the Brylisnki–Deligne data
(D,\eta)
to the cocharacter lattice
Y_{Q,n}
of
G_{Q,n}
and to the coroot lattice
Y_{Q,n}^{sc}
respectively to obtain an
n
-fold cover
\overline{G}_{Q,n}
. As the restriction of
B_{Q}
to
Y_{Q,n}
is trivial modulo
n
, the cover
\overline{G}_{Q,n}
is almost a trivial cover over
G_{Q,n}
. In particular, the covering torus
\overline{T}_{Q,n}\subset\overline{G}_{Q,n}
is abelian. In fact, we have

\overline{G}_{Q,n}^{\vee}=G_{Q,n}^{\vee}=\overline{G}^{\vee},

where
\overline{G}_{Q,n}^{\vee}
is the dual group for the
n
-fold cover
\overline{G}_{Q,n}
. One also has

Z(\overline{G}_{Q,n})=\overline{Z(G_{Q,n})}\cap Z(\overline{T}_{Q,n})=\overline{Z(G_{Q,n})}.

Recall the isogeny

i_{Q,n}:T_{Q,n}\longrightarrow T

induced from the inclusion
Y_{Q,n}\subset Y
. By pull-back, one naturally has the map (by abuse of notation) denoted by

i_{Q,n}:\overline{T}_{Q,n}\longrightarrow\overline{T}.

Lemma 3.1
.

Keeping notations as above, we have

(3.4)

i_{Q,n}(Z(\overline{G}_{Q,n}))=Z(\overline{G}).

Hence,
Z(\overline{G})^{\vee}=Z(G_{Q,n})^{\vee}
and the map
f_{G,z}:\overline{G}^{\vee}\longrightarrow Z(\overline{G})^{\vee}
is just

f_{G,z}:\overline{G}^{\vee}\longrightarrow\overline{G}^{\vee}/[\overline{G}^{\vee},\overline{G}^{\vee}],

where
[\overline{G}^{\vee},\overline{G}^{\vee}]\subset\overline{G}^{\vee}
is the derived subgroup.

Proof.

For simplicity, writing
i
for
i_{Q,n}
. One has a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X/X^{sc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{Q,n}/X_{Q,n}^{sc}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{Q,n},}

which gives the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(G_{Q,n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{Q,n}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

It follows that
i(Z(\overline{G}_{Q,n}))\subset Z(\overline{G})
. To show the other inclusion, we consider the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(G_{Q,n})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{Q,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Hom}(X_{Q,n}^{sc},F^{\times})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Ext}(X_{Q,n}/X_{Q,n}^{sc},F^{\times})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{z}\otimes F^{\times},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{z}}

where
i_{z}
is the map induced from the inclusion
Y_{z}\subset Y_{Q,n}
. It suffices to show that
r\circ i_{z}=0
. For every
y\otimes a\in Y_{z}\otimes F^{\times}
and
x\in X_{Q,n}^{sc}
, one has

r\circ i_{z}(y\otimes a)(x)=a^{{\left\langle x,y\right\rangle}},

where
{\left\langle-,-\right\rangle}:X_{Q,n}\times Y_{Q,n}\longrightarrow\mathbf{Z}
denotes the canonical pairing. However, since
x\in X_{Q,n}^{sc}
and
y\in Y_{c}\cap Y_{Q,n}
, we have
{\left\langle x,y\right\rangle}=0
. This shows that
r\circ i_{z}=0
and completes the proof of (
3.4
). It follows that
Z(\overline{G})^{\vee}=Z(G_{Q,n})^{\vee}
; as
\overline{G}^{\vee}=G_{Q,n}^{\vee}
, the rest is clear.
∎

To extend
f_{G,z}
to be an L-homomorphism. We choose a distinguished character

\chi_{\psi}:Z(\overline{T})\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

which gives an isomorphism
{}^{L}\overline{G}\simeq\overline{G}^{\vee}\times\text{WD}_{F}
, see §
2.1.3
. Note that by pull-back to
\overline{T}_{Q,n}\subset\overline{G}_{Q,n}
and further restriction to
Z(\overline{G}_{Q,n})
, one has a distinguished genuine character of
Z(\overline{G}_{Q,n})
, with respect to which we also have

{}^{L}Z(\overline{G})={}^{L}Z(\overline{G}_{Q,n})=Z(\overline{G})^{\vee}\times\text{WD}_{F}.

From this, we have an L-map as in (
3.3
).

Next, we want to understand the relation between
{}^{L}\overline{G}
and
{}^{L}\overline{G}_{0}
. Our exposition here follows closely that of
[
GG
, §12.3]
. Let
Y_{0}
be the cocharacter lattice of
G_{0}
. One has

Y^{sc}\subset Y_{0}\subset Y.

The root datum of
G_{0}
is

(X_{0},\Phi;Y_{0},\Phi^{\vee}),

where
X_{0}\supset X^{sc}
is the character lattice. By pull-back, one has a cover
\overline{G}_{0}
of
G_{0}
. Then dual group
\overline{G}_{0}^{\vee}
of
\overline{G}_{0}
has root datum

(Y_{0,Q,n},\Phi_{Q,n}^{\vee};X_{0,Q,n},\Phi_{Q,n}).

Unlike the linear algebraic case, there might be no group homomorphism from
\overline{G}^{\vee}
to
\overline{G}_{0}^{\vee}
in general. However, these two groups are related as follows.

First, we have

Y_{0}\cap Y_{Q,n}\subset Y_{0,Q,n}.

Let
{\bf H}
be the split algebraic group over
F
whose Langlands dual group
H^{\vee}
has root datum

(Y_{0}\cap Y_{Q,n},\ \Phi_{Q,n}^{\vee};\ \text{dual lattice of }Y_{0}\cap Y_{Q,n},\ \Phi_{Q,n}).

We obtain two homomorphisms of algebraic groups valued in
H^{\vee}

(3.5)

\overline{G}^{\vee}\longrightarrow H^{\vee}\longleftarrow\overline{G}_{0}^{\vee}.

By restricting the Brylinski–Deligne data
D
and
\eta
to
Y_{0}\cap Y_{Q,n}
and
Y_{Q,n}^{sc}
respectively, one obtains an
n
-fold cover
\overline{H}
of
H
such that

\overline{H}^{\vee}\simeq H^{\vee}\simeq\overline{G}^{\vee}.

Suppose there exist distinguished genuine characters for
\overline{G}
and
\overline{G}_{0}
, then we can extend the homomorphism in (
3.5
) to obtain two homomorphisms of L-groups,

f_{G,H}:{}^{L}\overline{G}\longrightarrow{}^{L}\overline{H}

and

f_{G_{0},H}:{}^{L}\overline{G}_{0}\longrightarrow{}^{L}\overline{H}.

Note that if
(\overline{G},\overline{G}_{0})
is an isotypic-pair, then

\overline{G}_{0}^{\vee}=\overline{H}^{\vee}.

In particular, this is true when
\overline{G}_{0}
is a saturated cover, i.e., when
\overline{G}_{0}^{\vee}
is of adjoint type; in this case,
f_{G_{0},H}
is an isomorphism.

3.2.
L-parameters and some speculations

To every parameter

\phi:\text{WD}_{F}\longrightarrow{}^{L}\overline{G}

we associate the group of connected components of the centralizer of
{\rm Im}(\phi)
:

\mathcal{S}_{\phi}=\mathcal{S}(\phi):=\pi_{0}\left({\rm Cent}_{\overline{G}^{\vee}}({\rm Im}(\phi)/Z(\overline{G}^{\vee}))\right).

Let
\Phi({}^{L}\overline{G})
be the set of
\overline{G}^{\vee}
-conjugacy classes of L-parameters of
\text{WD}_{F}
valued in
{}^{L}\overline{G}
. Let
\Phi^{\rm en}({}^{L}\overline{G})
be the set of enhanced parameters of the form

(\phi,\theta)\text{ with }\phi\in\Phi({}^{L}\overline{G})\text{ and }\theta\in{\rm Irr}(\mathcal{S}_{\phi}).

We assume that there is a local Langlands correspondence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi^{\rm en}({}^{L}\overline{G}),}

assigning to every
\pi\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{G})
a pair
(\phi_{\pi},\theta_{\pi})\in\Phi^{\rm en}({}^{L}\overline{G})
. For every
\phi\in\Phi({}^{L}\overline{G})
, denote by

\mathcal{L}(\phi):=\left\{\pi\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{G}):\ \phi_{\pi}=\phi\right\}\subset{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{G})

the hypothetical L-packet associated with
\phi
. Besides the one for linear algebraic groups given in
[
Bor
]
, there are several additional desiderata for such a correspondence as given in
[
GG
, §12]
, some of which we will also explain below. These desiderata encode natural properties expected on both the representation and parameter side.

We expect on the parameter side relations among various L-groups, and also the compatibility of certain L-parameters, as depicted in the following diagram.

(3.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{Z(G)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}Z(\overline{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{WD}_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{G}_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}H.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{c,z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\pi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\tau}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\rho}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{G,z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{G,H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{G_{o},H}}

To explain this, for every
\phi\in\Phi({}^{L}\overline{G})
, we denote

\phi^{\diamondsuit}=f_{G,H}\circ\phi

and set

\Phi({}^{L}\overline{G}_{0};\phi)=\left\{\phi_{0}\in\Phi({}^{L}\overline{G}_{0}):\ f_{G_{0},H}\circ\phi_{0}=\phi^{\diamondsuit}\right\}.

Consider the induced homomorphism

\mathcal{S}_{\phi}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\phi^{\diamondsuit}},

which is actually injective (see
[
Sol20
, Proposition 5.4]
). For every
\phi_{0}\in\Phi({}^{L}\overline{G}_{0};\phi)
, there is also an embedding

\mathcal{S}_{\phi_{0}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\phi^{\diamondsuit}}.

The following are some (refined) speculations from
[
GG
, §12.1–§12.3]
on central characters and restriction of representations to the derived subgroup.

Conjecture 3.2
.

Let
\pi\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{G})
be with central character
\omega_{\pi}
and associated enhanced parameter
(\phi_{\pi},\theta_{\pi})\in\Phi^{\rm en}({}^{L}\overline{G})
. Let
\tau\boxtimes\rho
be an irreducible representation of
\overline{G}^{\dagger}=\overline{Z(G)}\cdot\overline{G}_{0}
that occurs in the restriction of
\pi
.

(i)

The L-parameters
\phi_{\omega_{\pi}},\phi_{\tau}
and
\phi_{\rho}
satisfy the following:

\bullet

f_{G,z}\circ\phi_{\pi}=f_{c,z}\circ\phi_{\tau}
and is equal to the parameter
\phi_{\omega_{\pi}}
associated with the central character
\omega_{\pi}
of
\pi
;

\bullet

\phi_{\pi}^{\diamondsuit}=f_{G_{0},H}\circ\phi_{\rho}
.

(ii)

There exists
e(\pi)\in\mathbf{N}
such that for every
\tau\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{G}_{0})
, one has

\dim{\rm Hom}_{\overline{G}_{0}}(\pi,\tau)=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\phi_{\tau}\notin\Phi({}^{L}\overline{G}_{0};\phi_{\pi}),\\
e(\pi)\cdot{\left\langle{\rm Ind}_{\mathcal{S}_{\phi_{\pi}}}^{\mathcal{S}_{\phi^{\diamondsuit}}}\theta_{\pi},{\rm Ind}_{\mathcal{S}_{\phi_{\tau}}}^{\mathcal{S}_{\phi^{\diamondsuit}}}\theta_{\tau}\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{S}_{\phi^{\diamondsuit}}}&\text{ if }\phi_{\tau}\in\Phi({}^{L}\overline{G}_{0};\phi_{\pi}),\end{cases}

where
{\left\langle-,-\right\rangle}
denotes the pairing of two representations of
\mathcal{S}_{\phi^{\diamond}}
. In particular, if
(\overline{G},\overline{G}_{0})
is an isotypic-pair, then

\pi|_{\overline{G}_{0}}=e(\pi)\cdot\bigoplus_{\tau\in\mathcal{L}(\phi_{\pi}^{\diamondsuit})}{\left\langle\theta_{\tau}|_{\mathcal{S}_{\phi_{\pi}}},\theta_{\pi}\right\rangle}_{\mathcal{S}_{\phi_{\pi}}}\cdot\tau,

where
(\phi_{\pi}^{\diamondsuit},\theta_{\tau})\in\Phi^{\rm en}({}^{L}\overline{G}_{0})
is the enhanced parameter associated with
\tau\in\mathcal{L}(\phi_{\pi}^{\diamondsuit})
.

(iii)

Let
\tau\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{Z(G)})
and
\rho\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{G}_{0})
be compatible representations. Then every irreducible constituent
\pi\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(G)
of
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{G}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{G}}(\tau\boxtimes\rho)
has a parameter
\phi_{\pi}
which fits into a commutative diagram (
3.6
).

Part (ii) is clearly motivated from the case of linear algebraic groups, especially the work of Silberger
[
Sil79
]
, Keys
[
Key3
]
, Gelbart–Knapp
[
GeKn1
,
GeKn2
]
, Adler–Prasad
[
AP1
]
; see also that of Ban, Choiy and Goldberg
[
BCG18
,
Cho19
]
.

3.3.
Functoriality for genuine principal series

We first briefly recall the local Langlands correspondence for covering tori. The description here follows closely
[
We6
, §10]
. It gives the construction of the L-group
{}^{L}\overline{T}
of
\overline{T}
and establish the local Langlands correspondence for
\overline{T}
along the same line of the construction.

Let
T=Y\otimes F^{\times}
be a linear torus and
\overline{T}
an
n
-fold cover associated with a quadratic form
Q:Y\to\mathbf{Z}
. Let

i_{Q,n}:T_{Q,n}\to T

be the isogeny induced from the inclusion
Y_{Q,n}\subset Y
. Recall that the preimage of

T^{\dagger}:={\rm Im}(i_{Q,n})\subset T

inside
\overline{T}
is just the center
Z(\overline{T})
. Using the fixed embedding
\varepsilon:\mu_{n}\hookrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}
we obtain the push-out
\varepsilon_{*}(Z(\overline{T}))
of
Z(\overline{T})
. At the same time, any genuine character
\chi
of
Z(\overline{T})
gives rise to a splitting

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\chi}:\varepsilon_{*}(Z(\overline{T}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}^{\times}}

given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\chi}:[(z,\overline{t})]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z\cdot\chi^{-1}(\overline{t}),}

where
[(z,\overline{t})]
denote the class of

(z,\overline{t})\in\mathbf{C}^{\times}\times Z(\overline{T})

in
\varepsilon_{*}(Z(\overline{T}))
. We illustrate this by using the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\overline{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\dagger}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{*}(Z(\overline{T}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\dagger}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\chi}}

Clearly
s_{\chi}
entails a splitting
\phi_{\chi}
as in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{*}(Z(\overline{T}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T^{\dagger}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\chi}}

given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\chi}:\ t}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[(\chi(\overline{t}),\overline{t})]},}

where
\overline{t}\in Z(\overline{T})
is any lifting of
t\in\overline{T}^{\dagger}
.

By definition

\overline{T}^{\vee}=X_{Q,n}\otimes\mathbf{C}^{\times},

where
X_{Q,n}={\rm Hom}(Y_{Q,n},\mathbf{Z})
is the lattice dual to
Y_{Q,n}
. By abuse of notation, we still use
i_{Q,n}
to denote the naturally induced map

i_{Q,n}:X_{Q,n}\otimes T_{Q,n}\longrightarrow X_{Q,n}\otimes T^{\dagger}.

Consider the composite

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{m}:\text{WD}_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\times}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{Q,n}\otimes T_{Q,n}\simeq{\rm Hom}(Y_{Q,n},T_{Q,n}),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rm rec}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where the first map is the reciprocity map of class field theory sending a geometric Frobenius to the uniformizer
\varpi\in F^{\times}
and trivial on
{\rm SL}_{2}(\mathbf{C})\subset\text{WD}_{F}
, and the second map is given by

f(a)(y)=y\otimes{\rm rec}(a),\ y\in Y_{Q,n}.

The L-group
{}^{L}\overline{T}
is defined to be the pull-back of
X_{Q,n}\otimes\varepsilon_{*}(Z(\overline{T}))
via
i_{Q,n}\circ\mathfrak{m}
:

(3.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{T}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{Q,n}\otimes\varepsilon_{*}(Z(\overline{T}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{Q,n}\otimes T^{\dagger}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{T}^{\vee}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{WD}_{F}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\chi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{Q,n}\circ\mathfrak{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\chi}}

Here, the bottom splitting
s_{\chi}
of
{}^{L}\overline{T}
over
\text{WD}_{F}
is the one inherited from the splitting of the top extension.

The above construction

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi({}^{L}\overline{T})}

given by

i(\chi)\mapsto\phi_{\chi}

is the local Langlands correspondence (LLC) for covering torus, which is an injective map.

We want to obtain functoriality of LLC with respect to the restriction to a subgroup of
\overline{T}
, which is associated with a
W
-stable lattice
J\subset Y
. Note that
J_{Q,n}
may not be a sublattice of
Y_{Q,n}
in general, and we have

J_{Q,n}\supset(J\cap Y_{Q,n})\subset Y_{Q,n}.

Let
T_{J}
be the torus associated with
J
and
T_{J,\natural}
the torus associated with
J\cap Y_{Q,n}
. We have a relation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\overline{T}_{J})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\overline{T}_{J,\natural})=\overline{T}_{J,\natural}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\overline{T}).}

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
i(\chi_{J})\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{T}_{J})
and
i(\chi)\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{T})
. Then
i(\chi_{J})\subset i(\chi)|_{\overline{T}_{J}}
if and only if
\chi_{J}
and
\chi
agree on
\overline{T}_{J,\natural}
, or equivalently, the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\overline{T}_{J})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{T}_{J,\natural}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z(\overline{T})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{C}^{\times}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi_{J}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\chi}

commutes.

Proof.

The only if part is clear, and it suffices to prove the if part. Assume
\chi_{J}
and
\chi
agree on
\overline{T}_{J,\natural}
, then we get a genuine character

\chi_{J}\otimes\chi:\ Z(\overline{T}_{0})\cdot Z(\overline{T})\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}.

Let
\overline{A}_{0}\subset\overline{T}_{0}
be a maximal abelian subgroup of
\overline{T}_{0}
. We extend
\chi_{J}\otimes\chi
to a character

\chi_{0}^{\prime}\otimes\chi:\ \overline{A}_{0}\cdot Z(\overline{T})\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times},

where

\chi_{0}^{\prime}:\overline{A}_{0}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

is an extension of
\chi_{J}
. We can find a maximal abelian subgroup
\overline{A}\subset\overline{T}
containing
\overline{A}_{0}\cdot Z(\overline{T})
, and let

\chi^{\prime}:\ \overline{A}\longrightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}

be extending
\chi_{0}^{\prime}\otimes\chi
. Note that up to isomorphism class, we have

i(\chi_{J})\simeq{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}_{0}}^{\overline{T}_{0}}(\chi_{0}^{\prime})\text{ and }i(\chi)\simeq{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}}^{\overline{T}}(\chi^{\prime}).

Now Frobenius reciprocity gives that

{\rm Hom}_{\overline{T}_{0}}(i(\chi_{J}),i(\chi))={\rm Hom}_{\overline{A}_{0}}(\chi_{0}^{\prime},{\rm Ind}_{\overline{A}}^{\overline{T}}(\chi^{\prime}))\neq 0,

as
\chi^{\prime}
extends
\chi_{0}^{\prime}\otimes\chi
. This shows that
i(\chi_{J})\subset i(\chi)|_{\overline{T}_{0}}
.
∎

If
\chi_{J}
and
\chi
agree on
\overline{T}_{J,\natural}
, then we denote
\chi_{J,\natural}=\chi|_{\overline{T}_{J,\natural}}
. By the construction of L-groups, we have

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{T}_{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{T}_{J,\natural}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{T}.}

If
\chi
and
\chi_{J}
are compatible, then by the LLC for covering torus, the two parameters
\phi_{\chi_{J}}
and
\phi_{\chi}
are compatible, i.e., the following diagram commutes:

(3.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{T}_{J}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{T}_{J,\natural}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{WD}_{F},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\chi_{J}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\chi}}

where the middle vertical arrow is the parameter associated with
\chi_{J,\natural}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\pi:=I(\chi)
and
\rho:=I(\chi_{0})
be irreducible genuine principal series of
\overline{G}
and
\overline{G}_{0}
respectively. Let
\tau\in{\rm Irr}_{\rm gen}(\overline{Z(G)})
.

(i)

If
\tau\boxtimes\rho\in{\rm Irr}(\overline{G}^{\dagger})
occurs in
I(\chi)|_{\overline{G}^{\dagger}}
, then the L-parameters
\phi_{\tau}
and
\phi_{\rho}
are such that the diagram in (
3.6
) commutes. Moreover, if
{}^{L}\overline{G}_{0}={}^{L}\overline{H}
, then
I(\chi)|_{\overline{G}_{0}}
is an isotypic sum of a genuine principal series of
\overline{G}_{0}
.

(ii)

The parameter
\phi_{\pi}
of every irreducible constituent
\pi
of
{\rm Ind}_{\overline{G}^{\dagger}}^{\overline{G}}(\tau\boxtimes\rho)
satisfies a commutative diagram (
3.6
) involving
\phi_{\tau}
and
\phi_{\rho}
.

Proof.

For (i), we first consider
\phi_{\pi}
and
\phi_{\tau}
. Note that we have a natural map
{}^{L}\overline{T}\longrightarrow{}^{L}\overline{G}
, see §
2.1.3
. Taking
J=Y_{c}
, we thus have a commutative diagram from (
3.8
):

(3.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{Z(G)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{WD}_{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}Z(\overline{G})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}^{L}\overline{G}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi_{\tau}}
